Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You learn not to tell everybody your business, especially when you’ve seen how people talk at the grocery store or at church functions—one person says one thing and by the end of the day it’s all twisted around.

But long-term, like just suspicious of everyone I’m close to? I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that way. I’m careful, maybe. There’s a difference between being careful and being paranoid about people.

I don’t know… I mean, I don’t go around handing out every detail of my life, but I always thought that was just common sense. My mother used to say, “Don’t put your business in the street,” and she was right, because people do talk.

Like at church, you tell one person you’re having trouble with a bill or something, and next thing you know somebody’s cousin is asking if you’re “doing okay,” and they say it all sweet but you can tell they heard something. So I learned to keep certain things to myself.

But for many years because I thought everybody would use it against me? I don’t know if I’d say it like that. I’m private, that’s all. There’s some things people don’t need to know.

I can go to the store, talk to the cashier, talk to neighbors if I need to.\n\nIt’s just, when it’s family, you expect people to be straight with you, and that’s where it gets under my skin. My mother used to say you can tell more by what people don’t say than what they do say, and that’s true sometimes. But outside of this thing with my husband’s sister… I don’t know, I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel suspicious or mistrustful of family members besides your husband’s sister, like they might be trying to deceive you or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: I mean, family can be funny, you know? People sit at a table and smile, and then later you find out decisions got made without you. My aunt used to do that kind of thing years ago, always acting like she was “helping,” but somehow she got what she wanted.\n\nBut with this, it’s really my husband’s sister I’m talking about. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money, and she’s pushing me out of decisions. Other people try to smooth it over like I’m just upset or misunderstanding, but I know what I’ve been seeing. This has been going on more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself mistrusting others in situations outside of family matters, like at work, with friends, or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really at work or friends—well, I’m not working now, so I can’t say much about that. I used to know people who’d smile one way and talk another, but that’s just life, isn’t it?\n\nThe thing I came here for is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business, getting herself around the money and the decisions, trying to get control and push me out. Folks keep trying to smooth it over like I’m making too much of it, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people outside of your family, like friends or acquaintances, were trying to deceive or take advantage of you without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Friends or acquaintances? I mean, not like that, not without a reason. People can be selfish, sure, and I’ve known people who’ll take advantage if you let them, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions. Everybody wants to smooth it over and make it sound like I’m just reading into things, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your concerns with your husband’s sister and family matters, have you found yourself repeatedly suspicious of others’ intentions or doubting people’s trustworthiness over many years, even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. I mean, I’ve met people you can’t fully count on—everybody has, right? Like neighbors borrowing things and not bringing them back, or someone saying they’ll help and then suddenly they’re busy. That kind of thing sticks with you.\n\nBut over many years, just always doubting everybody? I don’t think so. I’m not walking around thinking every person is up to something. I just pay attention when something doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had long-term difficulties trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s motives in relationships, even when there wasn’t clear evidence, for reasons that aren’t connected to this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying. I mean, I’ve had people disappoint me, sure, but who hasn’t? You learn not to tell everybody your business, especially when you’ve seen how people talk at the grocery store or at church functions—one person says one thing and by the end of the day it’s all twisted around.\n\nBut long-term, like just suspicious of everyone I’m close to? I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that way. I’m careful, maybe. There’s a difference between being careful and being paranoid about people.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, been very cautious about sharing personal information with others because you're afraid it might be used against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t go around handing out every detail of my life, but I always thought that was just common sense. So I learned to keep certain things to myself.\n\nBut for many years because I thought everybody would use it against me? I don’t know if I’d say it like that. I’m private, that’s all. There’s some things people don’t need to know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I guess… not really like that. I mean, I don’t tell everybody everything, but that’s just how I was raised. My aunt used to say once you tell one person, you’ve told the whole block, and honestly, she wasn’t wrong.

But afraid they’d use it against me when there wasn’t a reason? I don’t know. Sometimes people repeat things without meaning harm, you know? Like they just can’t help themselves. I’ve had that happen with little things, nothing major. So I keep some stuff private.

This has been going on more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself mistrusting others in situations outside of family matters, like at work, with friends, or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really at work or friends—well, I’m not working now, so I can’t say much about that. I used to know people who’d smile one way and talk another, but that’s just life, isn’t it?\n\nThe thing I came here for is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business, getting herself around the money and the decisions, trying to get control and push me out. Folks keep trying to smooth it over like I’m making too much of it, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people outside of your family, like friends or acquaintances, were trying to deceive or take advantage of you without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Friends or acquaintances? I mean, not like that, not without a reason. People can be selfish, sure, and I’ve known people who’ll take advantage if you let them, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions. Everybody wants to smooth it over and make it sound like I’m just reading into things, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your concerns with your husband’s sister and family matters, have you found yourself repeatedly suspicious of others’ intentions or doubting people’s trustworthiness over many years, even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. I mean, I’ve met people you can’t fully count on—everybody has, right? Like neighbors borrowing things and not bringing them back, or someone saying they’ll help and then suddenly they’re busy. That kind of thing sticks with you.\n\nBut over many years, just always doubting everybody? I don’t think so. I’m not walking around thinking every person is up to something. I just pay attention when something doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had long-term difficulties trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s motives in relationships, even when there wasn’t clear evidence, for reasons that aren’t connected to this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying. I mean, I’ve had people disappoint me, sure, but who hasn’t? You learn not to tell everybody your business, especially when you’ve seen how people talk at the grocery store or at church functions—one person says one thing and by the end of the day it’s all twisted around.\n\nBut long-term, like just suspicious of everyone I’m close to? I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that way. I’m careful, maybe. There’s a difference between being careful and being paranoid about people.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, been very cautious about sharing personal information with others because you're afraid it might be used against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t go around handing out every detail of my life, but I always thought that was just common sense. My mother used to say, “Don’t put your business in the street,” and she was right, because people do talk.\n\nLike at church, you tell one person you’re having trouble with a bill or something, and next thing you know somebody’s cousin is asking if you’re “doing okay,” and they say it all sweet but you can tell they heard something. It’s mostly been around family, honestly… or really around my husband’s sister and the family business. My mother used to say you can tell more by what people don’t say than what they do say, and that’s true sometimes. But outside of this thing with my husband’s sister… I don’t know, I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel suspicious or mistrustful of family members besides your husband’s sister, like they might be trying to deceive you or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: I mean, family can be funny, you know? People sit at a table and smile, and then later you find out decisions got made without you. My aunt used to do that kind of thing years ago, always acting like she was “helping,” but somehow she got what she wanted.\n\nBut with this, it’s really my husband’s sister I’m talking about. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money, and she’s pushing me out of decisions. Other people try to smooth it over like I’m just upset or misunderstanding, but I know what I’ve been seeing. This has been going on more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself mistrusting others in situations outside of family matters, like at work, with friends, or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really at work or friends—well, I’m not working now, so I can’t say much about that. I used to know people who’d smile one way and talk another, but that’s just life, isn’t it?\n\nThe thing I came here for is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business, getting herself around the money and the decisions, trying to get control and push me out. Folks keep trying to smooth it over like I’m making too much of it, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people outside of your family, like friends or acquaintances, were trying to deceive or take advantage of you without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Friends or acquaintances? I mean, not like that, not without a reason. People can be selfish, sure, and I’ve known people who’ll take advantage if you let them, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions. Everybody wants to smooth it over and make it sound like I’m just reading into things, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your concerns with your husband’s sister and family matters, have you found yourself repeatedly suspicious of others’ intentions or doubting people’s trustworthiness over many years, even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. I mean, I’ve met people you can’t fully count on—everybody has, right? Like neighbors borrowing things and not bringing them back, or someone saying they’ll help and then suddenly they’re busy. That kind of thing sticks with you.\n\nBut over many years, just always doubting everybody? I don’t think so. I’m not walking around thinking every person is up to something. I just pay attention when something doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had long-term difficulties trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s motives in relationships, even when there wasn’t clear evidence, for reasons that aren’t connected to this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying. I mean, I’ve had people disappoint me, sure, but who hasn’t? You learn not to tell everybody your business, especially when you’ve seen how people talk at the grocery store or at church functions—one person says one thing and by the end of the day it’s all twisted around.\n\nBut long-term, like just suspicious of everyone I’m close to? I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that way. I’m careful, maybe. There’s a difference between being careful and being paranoid about people.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, been very cautious about sharing personal information with others because you're afraid it might be used against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t go around handing out every detail of my life, but I always thought that was just common sense. My mother used to say, “Don’t put your business in the street,” and she was right, because people do talk.\n\nLike at church, you tell one person you’re having trouble with a bill or something, and next thing you know somebody’s cousin is asking if you’re “doing okay,” and they say it all sweet but you can tell they heard something. So I learned to keep certain things to myself.\n\nBut for many years because I thought everybody would use it against me? I don’t know if I’d say it like that. I’m private, that’s all. There’s some things people don’t need to know.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, found yourself not confiding in others because you were afraid they might use that information against you, even when there was no clear reason to think they would?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really like that. I mean, I don’t tell everybody everything, but that’s just how I was raised. My aunt used to say once you tell one person, you’ve told the whole block, and honestly, she wasn’t wrong.\n\nBut afraid they’d use it against me when there wasn’t a reason? I don’t know. Sometimes people repeat things without meaning harm, you know? Like they just can’t help themselves. I’ve had that happen with little things, nothing major. So I keep some stuff private.\n\nI don’t think I’ve spent years thinking people were out to get me with whatever I told them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mistrust and suspicion of people taking advantage of you is something that has mainly happened with family members, or have you felt this way about people outside of your family too?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been around family, honestly… or really around my husband’s sister and the family business. That’s where all this started, because she’s been working things a certain way so she can get control of the money and edge me out of decisions. People keep trying to make it sound like it’s just “miscommunication” or “family stress,” but I know what I’ve been seeing.\n\nI mean, outside people can be nosy, sure—people at church, neighbors, everybody’s got opinions—but I’m not sitting around thinking the mailman or the cashier is trying to take advantage of me. This is different. This has been going on for more than a year, with her getting in people’s ears and acting like she’s just helping. That’s the part that bothers me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

No new information was provided that confirms or rules out either candidate definitively.", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really… I mean, families have their things. My side of the family, we’ve had arguments, people get their feelings hurt, somebody doesn’t come to Thanksgiving one year, then everybody acts like nothing happened by Easter. That’s just family to me.

I wouldn’t say I’ve gone around mistrusting all of them for years. Some people talk too much, yes, but that’s different. My cousin, for example, she’ll tell everybody’s business before the potato salad is even served, but I don’t think she’s plotting on me or anything.

So… no, not beyond normal family mess, I guess. This situation with my husband’s sister is its own thing.

But outside of this thing with my husband’s sister… I don’t know, I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel suspicious or mistrustful of family members besides your husband’s sister, like they might be trying to deceive you or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: I mean, family can be funny, you know? This has been going on more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself mistrusting others in situations outside of family matters, like at work, with friends, or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really at work or friends—well, I’m not working now, so I can’t say much about that. I used to know people who’d smile one way and talk another, but that’s just life, isn’t it?\n\nThe thing I came here for is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business, getting herself around the money and the decisions, trying to get control and push me out. Folks keep trying to smooth it over like I’m making too much of it, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people outside of your family, like friends or acquaintances, were trying to deceive or take advantage of you without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Friends or acquaintances? I mean, not like that, not without a reason. People can be selfish, sure, and I’ve known people who’ll take advantage if you let them, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions. Everybody wants to smooth it over and make it sound like I’m just reading into things, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your concerns with your husband’s sister and family matters, have you found yourself repeatedly suspicious of others’ intentions or doubting people’s trustworthiness over many years, even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. I mean, I’ve met people you can’t fully count on—everybody has, right? Like neighbors borrowing things and not bringing them back, or someone saying they’ll help and then suddenly they’re busy. That kind of thing sticks with you.\n\nBut over many years, just always doubting everybody? I don’t think so. I’m not walking around thinking every person is up to something. I just pay attention when something doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had long-term difficulties trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s motives in relationships, even when there wasn’t clear evidence, for reasons that aren’t connected to this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying. I mean, I’ve had people disappoint me, sure, but who hasn’t? You learn not to tell everybody your business, especially when you’ve seen how people talk at the grocery store or at church functions—one person says one thing and by the end of the day it’s all twisted around.\n\nBut long-term, like just suspicious of everyone I’m close to? I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that way. I’m careful, maybe. There’s a difference between being careful and being paranoid about people.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, been very cautious about sharing personal information with others because you're afraid it might be used against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t go around handing out every detail of my life, but I always thought that was just common sense. My mother used to say, “Don’t put your business in the street,” and she was right, because people do talk.\n\nLike at church, you tell one person you’re having trouble with a bill or something, and next thing you know somebody’s cousin is asking if you’re “doing okay,” and they say it all sweet but you can tell they heard something. Sometimes people repeat things without meaning harm, you know? Like they just can’t help themselves. I’ve had that happen with little things, nothing major. So I keep some stuff private.\n\nI don’t think I’ve spent years thinking people were out to get me with whatever I told them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mistrust and suspicion of people taking advantage of you is something that has mainly happened with family members, or have you felt this way about people outside of your family too?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been around family, honestly… or really around my husband’s sister and the family business. This is different. This has been going on for more than a year, with her getting in people’s ears and acting like she’s just helping. That’s the part that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, felt suspicious or mistrustful of other family members — not just your husband’s sister — in a way that went beyond just having disagreements or normal family tensions?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, families have their things. My side of the family, we’ve had arguments, people get their feelings hurt, somebody doesn’t come to Thanksgiving one year, then everybody acts like nothing happened by Easter. That’s just family to me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve gone around mistrusting all of them for years. Some people talk too much, yes, but that’s different. My cousin, for example, she’ll tell everybody’s business before the potato salad is even served, but I don’t think she’s plotting on me or anything.\n\nSo… no, not beyond normal family mess, I guess. This situation with my husband’s sister is its own thing.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you, for many years, found yourself suspecting that people were lying to you, cheating you, or planning something against you even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 52 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I came because my husband’s sister has been working this family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions, and no matter how folks try to smooth it over, I know what I’ve been seeing for more than a year now.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
For many years? No, I don’t think I’ve been like that with everybody. I’m not walking around thinking every person is lying to me or cheating me.

But with this thing with my husband’s sister, yes, I do believe she’s been working the family business in a way that gets her control of the money and pushes me out of decisions. And people keep saying, “Oh, maybe she didn’t mean it that way,” or “families get confused,” but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on over a year.

It’s like—well, my mother used to keep every receipt in a shoebox, so maybe I learned to pay attention to money, you know? But this isn’t me just being picky. There are things happening, and folks want me to act like I’m imagining it. I’m not.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because of what’s been going on with my husband’s sister. She’s been working this family business, you know, like she’s trying to get control of the money and push me out of the decisions. People keep trying to make it sound like it’s nothing, like “oh, that’s just family,” but I know what I’ve been seeing. This has been going on more than a year now. And it’s funny because family will smile at you at dinner and then turn around and act like you don’t notice things, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your husband's sister is intentionally trying to harm you or take advantage of you in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t think it’s an accident. She’s been working around that family business, getting herself in the middle of the money, and then somehow I’m the one left out of decisions. And everybody wants to smooth it over, like I’m making a fuss, but I’ve been watching it for over a year now. I can go to the store, talk to the cashier, talk to neighbors if I need to.\n\nIt’s just, when it’s family, you expect people to be straight with you, and that’s where it gets under my skin. My mother used to say you can tell more by what people don’t say than what they do say, and that’s true sometimes. But outside of this thing with my husband’s sister… I don’t know, I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel suspicious or mistrustful of family members besides your husband’s sister, like they might be trying to deceive you or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: I mean, family can be funny, you know? People sit at a table and smile, and then later you find out decisions got made without you. My aunt used to do that kind of thing years ago, always acting like she was “helping,” but somehow she got what she wanted.\n\nBut with this, it’s really my husband’s sister I’m talking about. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money, and she’s pushing me out of decisions. Other people try to smooth it over like I’m just upset or misunderstanding, but I know what I’ve been seeing. This has been going on more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself mistrusting others in situations outside of family matters, like at work, with friends, or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really at work or friends—well, I’m not working now, so I can’t say much about that. I used to know people who’d smile one way and talk another, but that’s just life, isn’t it?\n\nThe thing I came here for is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business, getting herself around the money and the decisions, trying to get control and push me out. Folks keep trying to smooth it over like I’m making too much of it, but I know what I’ve been seeing. I mean, not like that, not without a reason. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions. Everybody wants to smooth it over and make it sound like I’m just reading into things, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your concerns with your husband’s sister and family matters, have you found yourself repeatedly suspicious of others’ intentions or doubting people’s trustworthiness over many years, even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. I mean, I’ve met people you can’t fully count on—everybody has, right? Like neighbors borrowing things and not bringing them back, or someone saying they’ll help and then suddenly they’re busy. That kind of thing sticks with you.\n\nBut over many years, just always doubting everybody? I don’t think so. I’m not walking around thinking every person is up to something. I just pay attention when something doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had long-term difficulties trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s motives in relationships, even when there wasn’t clear evidence, for reasons that aren’t connected to this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying. I mean, I’ve had people disappoint me, sure, but who hasn’t? You learn not to tell everybody your business, especially when you’ve seen how people talk at the grocery store or at church functions—one person says one thing and by the end of the day it’s all twisted around.\n\nBut long-term, like just suspicious of everyone I’m close to? I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that way. I’m careful, maybe. There’s some things people don’t need to know.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, found yourself not confiding in others because you were afraid they might use that information against you, even when there was no clear reason to think they would?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really like that. I mean, I don’t tell everybody everything, but that’s just how I was raised. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Delusional Disorder. It’s been going on over a year.\n\nIt’s like—well, my mother used to keep every receipt in a shoebox, so maybe I learned to pay attention to money, you know? But this isn’t me just being picky. There are things happening, and folks want me to act like I’m imagining it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I don’t think so, not like that. I mean, relationships are complicated, you know? People say one thing and mean another sometimes, especially in families. My aunt used to say, “Watch what people do, not what they say,” and I guess that sticks with you.

But if you’re asking if I’ve always been suspicious of everybody I’ve been close to, no… I wouldn’t say that. I’ve had hurt feelings, sure. Friends disappoint you, people don’t call back, somebody borrows a dish and never returns it—little things like that. But I don’t sit there thinking everyone’s got a plan against me.

This thing with my husband’s sister is different. That’s the one I keep coming back to, because that’s where it doesn’t feel normal to me.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because of what’s been going on with my husband’s sister. She’s been working this family business, you know, like she’s trying to get control of the money and push me out of the decisions. People keep trying to make it sound like it’s nothing, like “oh, that’s just family,” but I know what I’ve been seeing. This has been going on more than a year now. And it’s funny because family will smile at you at dinner and then turn around and act like you don’t notice things, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your husband's sister is intentionally trying to harm you or take advantage of you in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t think it’s an accident. She’s been working around that family business, getting herself in the middle of the money, and then somehow I’m the one left out of decisions. And everybody wants to smooth it over, like I’m making a fuss, but I’ve been watching it for over a year now. You know, I grew up around people who would say one thing and do another, so I know when something’s being worked behind your back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your husband’s sister is part of a larger plan or group that is working against you, or is it something she is doing on her own?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a whole group, exactly. It’s her, my husband’s sister, she’s the one right there in the family business, putting herself around the money and the decisions. But then other folks act like they don’t see it, or they try to smooth it over, and that makes you wonder, you know?\n\nI came because this has been going on for more than a year, and I know what I’ve been seeing. She’s been trying to get control and push me out, and everybody wants to make it sound like just family stuff. But it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself mistrusting people in general, or feeling like others are trying to deceive or harm you, even outside of this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say people in general. I mean, I’m not walking around thinking every person is out to get me or anything. I can go to the store, talk to the cashier, talk to neighbors if I need to.\n\nIt’s just, when it’s family, you expect people to be straight with you, and that’s where it gets under my skin. My mother used to say you can tell more by what people don’t say than what they do say, and that’s true sometimes. But outside of this thing with my husband’s sister… I don’t know, I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel suspicious or mistrustful of family members besides your husband’s sister, like they might be trying to deceive you or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: I mean, family can be funny, you know? People sit at a table and smile, and then later you find out decisions got made without you. My aunt used to do that kind of thing years ago, always acting like she was “helping,” but somehow she got what she wanted.\n\nBut with this, it’s really my husband’s sister I’m talking about. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money, and she’s pushing me out of decisions. Other people try to smooth it over like I’m just upset or misunderstanding, but I know what I’ve been seeing. This has been going on more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself mistrusting others in situations outside of family matters, like at work, with friends, or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really at work or friends—well, I’m not working now, so I can’t say much about that. I used to know people who’d smile one way and talk another, but that’s just life, isn’t it?\n\nThe thing I came here for is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business, getting herself around the money and the decisions, trying to get control and push me out. Folks keep trying to smooth it over like I’m making too much of it, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people outside of your family, like friends or acquaintances, were trying to deceive or take advantage of you without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Friends or acquaintances? I mean, not like that, not without a reason. People can be selfish, sure, and I’ve known people who’ll take advantage if you let them, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions. Everybody wants to smooth it over and make it sound like I’m just reading into things, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your concerns with your husband’s sister and family matters, have you found yourself repeatedly suspicious of others’ intentions or doubting people’s trustworthiness over many years, even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. I mean, I’ve met people you can’t fully count on—everybody has, right? Like neighbors borrowing things and not bringing them back, or someone saying they’ll help and then suddenly they’re busy. That kind of thing sticks with you.\n\nBut over many years, just always doubting everybody? I don’t think so. I’m not walking around thinking every person is up to something. I just pay attention when something doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had long-term difficulties trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s motives in relationships, even when there wasn’t clear evidence, for reasons that aren’t connected to this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying. I mean, I’ve had people disappoint me, sure, but who hasn’t? You learn not to tell everybody your business, especially when you’ve seen how people talk at the grocery store or at church functions—one person says one thing and by the end of the day it’s all twisted around.\n\nBut long-term, like just suspicious of everyone I’m close to? I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that way. I’m careful, maybe. There’s a difference between being careful and being paranoid about people.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, been very cautious about sharing personal information with others because you're afraid it might be used against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t go around handing out every detail of my life, but I always thought that was just common sense. My mother used to say, “Don’t put your business in the street,” and she was right, because people do talk.\n\nLike at church, you tell one person you’re having trouble with a bill or something, and next thing you know somebody’s cousin is asking if you’re “doing okay,” and they say it all sweet but you can tell they heard something. So I learned to keep certain things to myself.\n\nBut for many years because I thought everybody would use it against me? I don’t know if I’d say it like that. I’m private, that’s all. There’s some things people don’t need to know.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, found yourself not confiding in others because you were afraid they might use that information against you, even when there was no clear reason to think they would?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really like that. I mean, I don’t tell everybody everything, but that’s just how I was raised. My aunt used to say once you tell one person, you’ve told the whole block, and honestly, she wasn’t wrong.\n\nBut afraid they’d use it against me when there wasn’t a reason? I don’t know. Sometimes people repeat things without meaning harm, you know? Like they just can’t help themselves. I’ve had that happen with little things, nothing major. So I keep some stuff private.\n\nI don’t think I’ve spent years thinking people were out to get me with whatever I told them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mistrust and suspicion of people taking advantage of you is something that has mainly happened with family members, or have you felt this way about people outside of your family too?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been around family, honestly… or really around my husband’s sister and the family business. That’s where all this started, because she’s been working things a certain way so she can get control of the money and edge me out of decisions. People keep trying to make it sound like it’s just “miscommunication” or “family stress,” but I know what I’ve been seeing.\n\nI mean, outside people can be nosy, sure—people at church, neighbors, everybody’s got opinions—but I’m not sitting around thinking the mailman or the cashier is trying to take advantage of me. This is different. This has been going on for more than a year, with her getting in people’s ears and acting like she’s just helping. That’s the part that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, felt suspicious or mistrustful of other family members — not just your husband’s sister — in a way that went beyond just having disagreements or normal family tensions?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, families have their things. That’s just family to me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve gone around mistrusting all of them for years. Some people talk too much, yes, but that’s different. No, I don’t think I’ve been like that with everybody. People can be selfish, sure, and I’ve known people who’ll take advantage if you let them, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions. Everybody wants to smooth it over and make it sound like I’m just reading into things, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your concerns with your husband’s sister and family matters, have you found yourself repeatedly suspicious of others’ intentions or doubting people’s trustworthiness over many years, even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. I mean, I’ve met people you can’t fully count on—everybody has, right? Like neighbors borrowing things and not bringing them back, or someone saying they’ll help and then suddenly they’re busy. That kind of thing sticks with you.\n\nBut over many years, just always doubting everybody? I don’t think so. I’m not walking around thinking every person is up to something. I just pay attention when something doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had long-term difficulties trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s motives in relationships, even when there wasn’t clear evidence, for reasons that aren’t connected to this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying. I mean, I’ve had people disappoint me, sure, but who hasn’t? You learn not to tell everybody your business, especially when you’ve seen how people talk at the grocery store or at church functions—one person says one thing and by the end of the day it’s all twisted around.\n\nBut long-term, like just suspicious of everyone I’m close to? I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that way. I’m careful, maybe. There’s a difference between being careful and being paranoid about people.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, been very cautious about sharing personal information with others because you're afraid it might be used against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t go around handing out every detail of my life, but I always thought that was just common sense. My mother used to say, “Don’t put your business in the street,” and she was right, because people do talk.\n\nLike at church, you tell one person you’re having trouble with a bill or something, and next thing you know somebody’s cousin is asking if you’re “doing okay,” and they say it all sweet but you can tell they heard something. So I learned to keep certain things to myself.\n\nBut for many years because I thought everybody would use it against me? I don’t know if I’d say it like that. I’m private, that’s all. There’s some things people don’t need to know.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, found yourself not confiding in others because you were afraid they might use that information against you, even when there was no clear reason to think they would?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really like that. I mean, I don’t tell everybody everything, but that’s just how I was raised. My aunt used to say once you tell one person, you’ve told the whole block, and honestly, she wasn’t wrong.\n\nBut afraid they’d use it against me when there wasn’t a reason? I don’t know. Sometimes people repeat things without meaning harm, you know? Like they just can’t help themselves. I’ve had that happen with little things, nothing major. So I keep some stuff private.\n\nI don’t think I’ve spent years thinking people were out to get me with whatever I told them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mistrust and suspicion of people taking advantage of you is something that has mainly happened with family members, or have you felt this way about people outside of your family too?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been around family, honestly… or really around my husband’s sister and the family business. That’s where all this started, because she’s been working things a certain way so she can get control of the money and edge me out of decisions. People keep trying to make it sound like it’s just “miscommunication” or “family stress,” but I know what I’ve been seeing.\n\nI mean, outside people can be nosy, sure—people at church, neighbors, everybody’s got opinions—but I’m not sitting around thinking the mailman or the cashier is trying to take advantage of me. This is different. This has been going on for more than a year, with her getting in people’s ears and acting like she’s just helping. That’s the part that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, felt suspicious or mistrustful of other family members — not just your husband’s sister — in a way that went beyond just having disagreements or normal family tensions?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, families have their things. My side of the family, we’ve had arguments, people get their feelings hurt, somebody doesn’t come to Thanksgiving one year, then everybody acts like nothing happened by Easter. That’s just family to me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve gone around mistrusting all of them for years. Some people talk too much, yes, but that’s different. My cousin, for example, she’ll tell everybody’s business before the potato salad is even served, but I don’t think she’s plotting on me or anything.\n\nSo… no, not beyond normal family mess, I guess. This situation with my husband’s sister is its own thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, found yourself suspecting that people were lying to you, cheating you, or planning something against you even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: For many years? No, I don’t think I’ve been like that with everybody. I’m not walking around thinking every person is lying to me or cheating me.\n\nBut with this thing with my husband’s sister, yes, I do believe she’s been working the family business in a way that gets her control of the money and pushes me out of decisions. And people keep saying, “Oh, maybe she didn’t mean it that way,” or “families get confused,” but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on over a year.\n\nIt’s like—well, my mother used to keep every receipt in a shoebox, so maybe I learned to pay attention to money, you know? But this isn’t me just being picky. There are things happening, and folks want me to act like I’m imagining it. I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, had trouble trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s intentions in relationships — even when there wasn’t clear proof — in ways that were not related to your concerns about your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think so, not like that. I mean, relationships are complicated, you know? People say one thing and mean another sometimes, especially in families. My aunt used to say, “Watch what people do, not what they say,” and I guess that sticks with you.\n\nBut if you’re asking if I’ve always been suspicious of everybody I’ve been close to, no… I wouldn’t say that. I’ve had hurt feelings, sure. Friends disappoint you, people don’t call back, somebody borrows a dish and never returns it—little things like that. But I don’t sit there thinking everyone’s got a plan against me.\n\nThis thing with my husband’s sister is different. That’s the one I keep coming back to, because that’s where it doesn’t feel normal to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  "question": "Have you, for many years, had difficulty trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s intentions in situations outside of your family, even when there wasn’t clear proof?"

Outside my family? Um… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really noticed that. I mean, I’m careful with people, sure, like if somebody at the store shortchanges you or a neighbor says they’ll do something and then doesn’t—but that’s just life, isn’t it?

But if you mean do I go around thinking people outside the family are out to get me, no… not really. I can’t say I’ve had that for years or anything.

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I’m careful, sure, but I don’t think I’ve spent years mistrusting people outside the family like that.

Sometimes you get a bad feeling about someone, like a salesperson talking too fast, or somebody promising they’ll fix something and then suddenly the price changes—my husband always hated dealing with repair people, actually, he’d say, “Get it in writing,” and he wasn’t wrong. But that’s just being sensible, isn’t it?

So… no, I haven’t really noticed that as some ongoing thing with people outside the family.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22.0, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because of what’s been going on with my husband’s sister. She’s been working this family business, you know, like she’s trying to get control of the money and push me out of the decisions. People keep trying to make it sound like it’s nothing, like “oh, that’s just family,” but I know what I’ve been seeing. This has been going on more than a year now. And it’s funny because family will smile at you at dinner and then turn around and act like you don’t notice things, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your husband's sister is intentionally trying to harm you or take advantage of you in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I don’t think it’s an accident. She’s been working around that family business, getting herself in the middle of the money, and then somehow I’m the one left out of decisions. And everybody wants to smooth it over, like I’m making a fuss, but I’ve been watching it for over a year now. You know, I grew up around people who would say one thing and do another, so I know when something’s being worked behind your back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your husband’s sister is part of a larger plan or group that is working against you, or is it something she is doing on her own?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a whole group, exactly. It’s her, my husband’s sister, she’s the one right there in the family business, putting herself around the money and the decisions. But then other folks act like they don’t see it, or they try to smooth it over, and that makes you wonder, you know?\n\nI came because this has been going on for more than a year, and I know what I’ve been seeing. She’s been trying to get control and push me out, and everybody wants to make it sound like just family stuff. But it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself mistrusting people in general, or feeling like others are trying to deceive or harm you, even outside of this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say people in general. I mean, I’m not walking around thinking every person is out to get me or anything. I can go to the store, talk to the cashier, talk to neighbors if I need to.\n\nIt’s just, when it’s family, you expect people to be straight with you, and that’s where it gets under my skin. My mother used to say you can tell more by what people don’t say than what they do say, and that’s true sometimes. But outside of this thing with my husband’s sister… I don’t know, I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel suspicious or mistrustful of family members besides your husband’s sister, like they might be trying to deceive you or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: I mean, family can be funny, you know? People sit at a table and smile, and then later you find out decisions got made without you. My aunt used to do that kind of thing years ago, always acting like she was “helping,” but somehow she got what she wanted.\n\nBut with this, it’s really my husband’s sister I’m talking about. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money, and she’s pushing me out of decisions. Other people try to smooth it over like I’m just upset or misunderstanding, but I know what I’ve been seeing. This has been going on more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself mistrusting others in situations outside of family matters, like at work, with friends, or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really at work or friends—well, I’m not working now, so I can’t say much about that. I used to know people who’d smile one way and talk another, but that’s just life, isn’t it?\n\nThe thing I came here for is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business, getting herself around the money and the decisions, trying to get control and push me out. Folks keep trying to smooth it over like I’m making too much of it, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people outside of your family, like friends or acquaintances, were trying to deceive or take advantage of you without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Friends or acquaintances? I mean, not like that, not without a reason. People can be selfish, sure, and I’ve known people who’ll take advantage if you let them, but that’s not really what brought me here.\n\nWhat I’m talking about is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions. Everybody wants to smooth it over and make it sound like I’m just reading into things, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your concerns with your husband’s sister and family matters, have you found yourself repeatedly suspicious of others’ intentions or doubting people’s trustworthiness over many years, even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. I mean, I’ve met people you can’t fully count on—everybody has, right? Like neighbors borrowing things and not bringing them back, or someone saying they’ll help and then suddenly they’re busy. That kind of thing sticks with you.\n\nBut over many years, just always doubting everybody? I don’t think so. I’m not walking around thinking every person is up to something. I just pay attention when something doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had long-term difficulties trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s motives in relationships, even when there wasn’t clear evidence, for reasons that aren’t connected to this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying. I mean, I’ve had people disappoint me, sure, but who hasn’t? You learn not to tell everybody your business, especially when you’ve seen how people talk at the grocery store or at church functions—one person says one thing and by the end of the day it’s all twisted around.\n\nBut long-term, like just suspicious of everyone I’m close to? I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that way. I’m careful, maybe. There’s a difference between being careful and being paranoid about people.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, been very cautious about sharing personal information with others because you're afraid it might be used against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t go around handing out every detail of my life, but I always thought that was just common sense. My mother used to say, “Don’t put your business in the street,” and she was right, because people do talk.\n\nLike at church, you tell one person you’re having trouble with a bill or something, and next thing you know somebody’s cousin is asking if you’re “doing okay,” and they say it all sweet but you can tell they heard something. So I learned to keep certain things to myself.\n\nBut for many years because I thought everybody would use it against me? I don’t know if I’d say it like that. I’m private, that’s all. There’s some things people don’t need to know.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, found yourself not confiding in others because you were afraid they might use that information against you, even when there was no clear reason to think they would?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really like that. I mean, I don’t tell everybody everything, but that’s just how I was raised. My aunt used to say once you tell one person, you’ve told the whole block, and honestly, she wasn’t wrong.\n\nBut afraid they’d use it against me when there wasn’t a reason? I don’t know. Sometimes people repeat things without meaning harm, you know? Like they just can’t help themselves. I’ve had that happen with little things, nothing major. So I keep some stuff private.\n\nI don’t think I’ve spent years thinking people were out to get me with whatever I told them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mistrust and suspicion of people taking advantage of you is something that has mainly happened with family members, or have you felt this way about people outside of your family too?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been around family, honestly… or really around my husband’s sister and the family business. That’s where all this started, because she’s been working things a certain way so she can get control of the money and edge me out of decisions. People keep trying to make it sound like it’s just “miscommunication” or “family stress,” but I know what I’ve been seeing.\n\nI mean, outside people can be nosy, sure—people at church, neighbors, everybody’s got opinions—but I’m not sitting around thinking the mailman or the cashier is trying to take advantage of me. This is different. This has been going on for more than a year, with her getting in people’s ears and acting like she’s just helping. That’s the part that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, felt suspicious or mistrustful of other family members — not just your husband’s sister — in a way that went beyond just having disagreements or normal family tensions?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, families have their things. My side of the family, we’ve had arguments, people get their feelings hurt, somebody doesn’t come to Thanksgiving one year, then everybody acts like nothing happened by Easter. That’s just family to me.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve gone around mistrusting all of them for years. Some people talk too much, yes, but that’s different. My cousin, for example, she’ll tell everybody’s business before the potato salad is even served, but I don’t think she’s plotting on me or anything.\n\nSo… no, not beyond normal family mess, I guess. This situation with my husband’s sister is its own thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, found yourself suspecting that people were lying to you, cheating you, or planning something against you even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: For many years? No, I don’t think I’ve been like that with everybody. I’m not walking around thinking every person is lying to me or cheating me.\n\nBut with this thing with my husband’s sister, yes, I do believe she’s been working the family business in a way that gets her control of the money and pushes me out of decisions. And people keep saying, “Oh, maybe she didn’t mean it that way,” or “families get confused,” but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on over a year.\n\nIt’s like—well, my mother used to keep every receipt in a shoebox, so maybe I learned to pay attention to money, you know? But this isn’t me just being picky. There are things happening, and folks want me to act like I’m imagining it. I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, had trouble trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s intentions in relationships — even when there wasn’t clear proof — in ways that were not related to your concerns about your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think so, not like that. I mean, relationships are complicated, you know? People say one thing and mean another sometimes, especially in families. My aunt used to say, “Watch what people do, not what they say,” and I guess that sticks with you.\n\nBut if you’re asking if I’ve always been suspicious of everybody I’ve been close to, no… I wouldn’t say that. I’ve had hurt feelings, sure. Friends disappoint you, people don’t call back, somebody borrows a dish and never returns it—little things like that. But I don’t sit there thinking everyone’s got a plan against me.\n\nThis thing with my husband’s sister is different. That’s the one I keep coming back to, because that’s where it doesn’t feel normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, had difficulty trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s intentions in situations outside of your family, even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: Outside my family? Um… I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really noticed that. I mean, I’m careful with people, sure, like if somebody at the store shortchanges you or a neighbor says they’ll do something and then doesn’t—but that’s just life, isn’t it?\n\nI used to have this lady down the street who would borrow little things, sugar, a pan, whatever, and then act like she forgot. My mother would’ve said, “Don’t keep lending what you can’t afford to lose.” So I guess I pay attention.\n\nBut if you mean do I go around thinking people outside the family are out to get me, no… not really. I can’t say I’ve had that for years or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, felt suspicious or mistrustful of people outside of your family, even when there wasn’t clear proof they were trying to deceive or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, I guess. I’ve had neighbors I didn’t care for, sure. One woman used to let her dog run loose and then act surprised when it got into people’s yards, and that kind of thing makes you wonder about folks’ manners, but that’s not the same as thinking they’re doing something to me.

Friends… I mean, people can be funny. They say they’ll come by and then don’t, or they get busy. I might feel hurt or annoyed, but I don’t sit around thinking they’re all against me.

So, no… I haven’t really noticed being suspicious like that for years with people I’m not related to. At least not that I can think of.

I mean, I’m not walking around thinking every person is out to get me or anything. I can go to the store, talk to the cashier, talk to neighbors if I need to.\n\nIt’s just, when it’s family, you expect people to be straight with you, and that’s where it gets under my skin. My mother used to say you can tell more by what people don’t say than what they do say, and that’s true sometimes. But outside of this thing with my husband’s sister… I don’t know, I haven’t really thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel suspicious or mistrustful of family members besides your husband’s sister, like they might be trying to deceive you or take advantage of you?\n\nPatient: I mean, family can be funny, you know? People sit at a table and smile, and then later you find out decisions got made without you. My aunt used to do that kind of thing years ago, always acting like she was “helping,” but somehow she got what she wanted.\n\nBut with this, it’s really my husband’s sister I’m talking about. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money, and she’s pushing me out of decisions. This has been going on more than a year.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself mistrusting others in situations outside of family matters, like at work, with friends, or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really at work or friends—well, I’m not working now, so I can’t say much about that. I used to know people who’d smile one way and talk another, but that’s just life, isn’t it?\n\nThe thing I came here for is my husband’s sister. She’s been working that family business, getting herself around the money and the decisions, trying to get control and push me out. Folks keep trying to smooth it over like I’m making too much of it, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people outside of your family, like friends or acquaintances, were trying to deceive or take advantage of you without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Friends or acquaintances? I mean, not like that, not without a reason. She’s been working that family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions. Everybody wants to smooth it over and make it sound like I’m just reading into things, but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your concerns with your husband’s sister and family matters, have you found yourself repeatedly suspicious of others’ intentions or doubting people’s trustworthiness over many years, even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. I mean, I’ve met people you can’t fully count on—everybody has, right? Like neighbors borrowing things and not bringing them back, or someone saying they’ll help and then suddenly they’re busy. That kind of thing sticks with you.\n\nBut over many years, just always doubting everybody? I don’t think so. I’m not walking around thinking every person is up to something. I just pay attention when something doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had long-term difficulties trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s motives in relationships, even when there wasn’t clear evidence, for reasons that aren’t connected to this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying. I mean, I’ve had people disappoint me, sure, but who hasn’t? You learn not to tell everybody your business, especially when you’ve seen how people talk at the grocery store or at church functions—one person says one thing and by the end of the day it’s all twisted around.\n\nBut long-term, like just suspicious of everyone I’m close to? I don’t know. I wouldn’t put it that way. I’m careful, maybe. There’s a difference between being careful and being paranoid about people.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, been very cautious about sharing personal information with others because you're afraid it might be used against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t go around handing out every detail of my life, but I always thought that was just common sense. My mother used to say, “Don’t put your business in the street,” and she was right, because people do talk.\n\nLike at church, you tell one person you’re having trouble with a bill or something, and next thing you know somebody’s cousin is asking if you’re “doing okay,” and they say it all sweet but you can tell they heard something. So I learned to keep certain things to myself.\n\nBut for many years because I thought everybody would use it against me? I don’t know if I’d say it like that. I’m private, that’s all. There’s some things people don’t need to know.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, found yourself not confiding in others because you were afraid they might use that information against you, even when there was no clear reason to think they would?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really like that. Sometimes people repeat things without meaning harm, you know? Like they just can’t help themselves. I’ve had that happen with little things, nothing major. So I keep some stuff private.\n\nI don’t think I’ve spent years thinking people were out to get me with whatever I told them, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mistrust and suspicion of people taking advantage of you is something that has mainly happened with family members, or have you felt this way about people outside of your family too?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly been around family, honestly… or really around my husband’s sister and the family business. That’s where all this started, because she’s been working things a certain way so she can get control of the money and edge me out of decisions. People keep trying to make it sound like it’s just “miscommunication” or “family stress,” but I know what I’ve been seeing.\n\nI mean, outside people can be nosy, sure—people at church, neighbors, everybody’s got opinions—but I’m not sitting around thinking the mailman or the cashier is trying to take advantage of me. But it’s not nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself mistrusting people in general, or feeling like others are trying to deceive or harm you, even outside of this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say people in general. She’s been working that family business, getting herself around the money and the decisions, trying to get control and push me out. It’s been more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that people outside of your family, like friends or acquaintances, were trying to deceive or take advantage of you without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: Friends or acquaintances? It’s been going on more than a year now.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your concerns with your husband’s sister and family matters, have you found yourself repeatedly suspicious of others’ intentions or doubting people’s trustworthiness over many years, even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that, no. That kind of thing sticks with you.\n\nBut over many years, just always doubting everybody? I don’t think so. I’m not walking around thinking every person is up to something. I just pay attention when something doesn’t feel right.\n\nDoctor: Have you had long-term difficulties trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s motives in relationships, even when there wasn’t clear evidence, for reasons that aren’t connected to this situation with your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re saying. I mean, I’ve had people disappoint me, sure, but who hasn’t? You learn not to tell everybody your business, especially when you’ve seen how people talk at the grocery store or at church functions—one person says one thing and by the end of the day it’s all twisted around.\n\nBut long-term, like just suspicious of everyone I’m close to? This situation with my husband’s sister is its own thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, found yourself suspecting that people were lying to you, cheating you, or planning something against you even when there wasn’t clear proof?\n\nPatient: For many years? No, I don’t think I’ve been like that with everybody. I’m not walking around thinking every person is lying to me or cheating me.\n\nBut with this thing with my husband’s sister, yes, I do believe she’s been working the family business in a way that gets her control of the money and pushes me out of decisions. And people keep saying, “Oh, maybe she didn’t mean it that way,” or “families get confused,” but I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s been going on over a year.\n\nIt’s like—well, my mother used to keep every receipt in a shoebox, so maybe I learned to pay attention to money, you know? But this isn’t me just being picky. I’m not.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, had trouble trusting others or felt suspicious of people’s intentions in relationships — even when there wasn’t clear proof — in ways that were not related to your concerns about your husband’s sister?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think so, not like that. But that’s just being sensible, isn’t it?\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as some ongoing thing with people outside the family.\n\nDoctor: Have you, for many years, found yourself being wary or suspicious of people you aren’t related to—like friends, neighbors, or coworkers—even when there wasn’t a clear reason to feel that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, I guess. I’ve had neighbors I didn’t care for, sure. They say they’ll come by and then don’t, or they get busy. I might feel hurt or annoyed, but I don’t sit around thinking they’re all against me.\n\nSo, no… I haven’t really noticed being suspicious like that for years with people I’m not related to.
